<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove that the infinite, ordinarily finitely presented periodic Steinberg group $`\Gamma=\mathop{\mathrm{St}}\nolimits _{12}(R)`$ of a companion paper is residually finite. Its finite-index subgroup $`G=\ker(\Gamma\to\mathop{\mathrm{St}}\nolimits _{12}(\mathbb F_2))`$ is infinite, ordinarily finitely presented, and residually finite, and every element of <i>G</i> has finite 2-power order. The orders of its elements are unbounded. Phases of long words in the companion's graded algebra allow finite degree truncations to detect all elements of Γ, including the central kernel.

- **C1** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): There exists an infinite residually finite group with an ordinary finite presentation in which every element has finite 2-power order.
  - Explanation: Establishes an infinite residually finite group with an ordinary finite presentation and finite element orders that are powers of two.
- **C2** (construction, [source](#stmt-prop-ring-input)): There is an infinite finitely presented unital associative algebra $$R=\bigoplus_{d\geq0}R_d=\mathbb F_2\oplus I, \qquad R_0=\mathbb F_2, \qquad I=\bigoplus_{d\geq1}R_d,$$ such that every $R_d$ is finite-dimensional and $I$ is matrix-nil. This algebra has the word presentation and hierarchy specified in Proposition <a href="#prop:hierarchy-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:hierarchy-input">3</a>.
  - Explanation: Supplies the graded companion algebra with finite-dimensional homogeneous components and a matrix-nil positive-degree ideal.
- **C3** (construction, [source](#stmt-prop-hierarchy-input)): The algebra $R$ in Proposition <a href="#prop:ring-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:ring-input">2</a> is the first algebra $A_0$ of a sequence $(A_j)_{j\geq0}$ with the following properties. There are positive integers $c,k,h_0,D$, with $D>10(c+1)(k+1)$, and $$h_j=h_0D^j,\qquad L_j=2\cdot2^{h_j}+1.$$ For each $j\geq0$:* 1. *$A_j$ is presented by a finite alphabet $\Sigma_j$, finitely many replacements, and finitely many zero declarations, all of length at most $k$. It is graded by word length.* 2. *There is a substitution $E_j$ sending each letter of $\Sigma_{j+1}$ to a word of length $L_j$ over $\Sigma_j$. Extended by concatenation, it induces a unital algebra homomorphism $A_{j+1}\to A_j$.* 3. *A specified subset of $\Sigma_j$ gives cuts immediately before its letters. There are integers $B_j,T_j\geq0$ and a finite set of words $\mathcal T_j$ such that every nonzero word $w$ of length at least $T_j$ has the unique decomposition $$\label{eq:token-decomposition} w=p_w\,t_1\cdots t_r\,s_w, \qquad \|p_w\|,\|s_w\|\leq B_j,\qquad t_i\in\mathcal T_j.$$ Here $p_w$ precedes the first cut, $s_w$ begins at the last cut, and the $t_i$ are exactly the intervals between consecutive cuts. A cut is counted only if its following letter occurs in the word. For each $t\in\mathcal T_j$ there is a nonempty word $u_t$ over $\Sigma_{j+1}$ such that $$\label{eq:token-upper-word} 1\leq \|u_t\|\leq k,\qquad \|t\|=L_j\|u_t\|,\qquad [t]=[E_j(u_t)]\quad\hbox{in }A_j.$$
  - Explanation: States the finite word rules, substitutions, and bounded token decompositions used to recover phases.
- **C4** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-phases)): For every integer $d\geq0$, put $$L=L_0L_1\cdots L_d.$$ There is an integer $N\geq1$ such that every nonzero word $w$ in $R$ of length at least $N$ has a start phase $\sigma(w)\in\mathbb Z/L\mathbb Z$ and an end phase $$\tau(w)=\sigma(w)+\|w\|\in\mathbb Z/L\mathbb Z$$ with the following properties:* 1. *Both phases depend only on the basis class of $w$.* 2. *If $v$ is a contiguous subword of $w$, has length at least $N$, and starts at offset $b$ from the beginning of $w$, then $$\label{eq:phase-subword} \sigma(v)=\sigma(w)+b.$$* *The integers $L$ so obtained are odd and arbitrarily large.
  - Explanation: Constructs class-invariant start and end phases with the subword offset law and unbounded odd moduli.
- **C5** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-cor-phase-products)): Fix $L,N,\sigma,\tau$ from Lemma <a href="#lem:phases" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:phases">4</a>. If $u,v$ are nonzero basis words of lengths at least $N$, then $$\tau(u)\ne\sigma(v)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad uv=0.$$ If $uv\ne0$, its phases satisfy $\sigma(uv)=\sigma(u)$ and $\tau(uv)=\tau(v)$.
  - Explanation: Makes a phase mismatch a sufficient condition for a product of long basis words to vanish.
- **C6** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-strip)): Let $S,T$ be disjoint finite sets of coordinates. The subgroup generated by $x_{ij}(a)$ with $i\in S$, $j\in T$ maps isomorphically onto the additive group of matrices supported in $S\times T$ under $u\mapsto \pi(u)-I$. A word supported on $S$ normalizes this subgroup and acts on its entries by left multiplication by its matrix on $S$. On the reverse strip $T\times S$, its action is right multiplication by the inverse matrix on $S$.
  - Explanation: Identifies disjoint root strips with additive matrix entries and gives exact conjugation actions.
- **C7** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-stable-center)): For every $B$, the group $J(B)$ is central in $\mathop{\mathrm{St}}(B)$.
  - Explanation: Shows that the stable kernel of the matrix map is central.
- **C8** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-permutations)): In characteristic two, every permutation of a finite set of coordinates is induced on roots by conjugation in the Steinberg group after adjoining an outside coordinate if necessary. In particular, relabeling the finitely many coordinates in a representative of $z\in J(B)$ leaves the element $z$ unchanged in $\mathop{\mathrm{St}}(B)$.
  - Explanation: Realizes coordinate permutations by conjugation and preserves stable central elements under relabeling.
- **C9** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-commutator)): In any group, let $A=[X,Y]$ and $B'=[Y,Z]$. If $$[X,Z]=[A,X]=[B',Z]=[A,B']=1,$$ then $[A,Z]=[X,B']$. This common commutator commutes with $A,Z,X,B'$.
  - Explanation: Derives a commutator identity under explicit commuting hypotheses.
- **C10** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-block-laws)): The block roots satisfy the Steinberg relations over $\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}_b(B)$: $$\begin{aligned} X_{ij}(A+A')&=X_{ij}(A)X_{ij}(A'),\\ [X_{ij}(A),X_{kl}(A')]&=1 &&\text{if }j\ne k\text{ and }i\ne l,\\ [X_{ij}(A),X_{jk}(A')]&=X_{ik}(AA') &&\text{if }i,j,k\text{ are distinct}. \end{aligned}$$ Consequently they define a homomorphism $\mathop{\mathrm{St}}_n(\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}_b(B))\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{St}}_{nb}(B)$. Moreover, for every $P\in\mathop{\mathrm{GL}}_b(\mathbb F_2)$ there is an element $q\in\mathop{\mathrm{St}}_{nb}(B)$ such that $$q^{-1}X_{ij}(A)q=X_{ij}(P^{-1}AP) \qquad(i\ne j).$$ Its matrix image is the block diagonal matrix with a copy of $P$ in every outer block.
  - Explanation: Constructs matrix-ring block roots and lifts simultaneous numerical changes of basis.
- **C11** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-triangular)): Let $U$ be generated by the roots $x_{(i,s),(j,t)}(a)$ with $s<t$, allowing $i=j$. Then $U$ injects into its matrix image and is normalized by every root whose inner indices agree. If a root word $v(t)$ over $B[t]$ has identity matrix image, and $T\in\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}_b(\mathbb F_2)$ is upper triangular with constant diagonal entry $\lambda$, then $$\label{eq:triangular-evaluation} W_v(T)=\prod_{s=0}^{b-1}\iota_s\bigl(v(\lambda)\bigr).$$
  - Explanation: Proves faithfulness of the positive-gap subgroup and factors triangular evaluations into diagonal coordinate images.
- **C12** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-wrap)): Let $v(t)$ be a product of $m\geq1$ root factors over $B[t]$, whose polynomial entries have degrees at most $d\geq0$. Suppose its matrix image is the identity over $B[t]$. Put $\delta=\max\{1,d\}$. If $$b>2(m+1)\delta,$$ then $W_v(C_b)=W_v(S_b)$ in $\mathop{\mathrm{St}}_{nb}(B)$.
  - Explanation: Removes the cyclic wrap when the correction bands remain disjoint.
- **C13** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-cyclic-vanishing)): If $z\in J(R)$ maps to $1$ in $\mathop{\mathrm{St}}(\mathbb F_2)$, then $$W_z(C_b)=1 \qquad\text{for every sufficiently large integer }b.$$ More precisely, if the graded word $z(t)$ has $m\geq1$ factors of degrees at most $d$, it suffices that $b>2(m+1)\max\{1,d\}$.
  - Explanation: Proves vanishing of cyclic evaluation for relative central elements at sufficiently large shift dimensions.
- **C14** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-relative-torsion)): Every element of $$J(R)\cap\ker\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{St}}(R)\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{St}}(\mathbb F_2)\bigr)$$ has finite $2$-power order.
  - Explanation: Deduces finite order that is a power of two in the relative stable central kernel.
- **C15** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-opposite)): Suppose $a,c\in R$ satisfy $ca=0$. For distinct indices $i,h,j$ and every $b\in R$, $$\label{eq:cut-movement} [x_{ij}(ab),x_{ji}(c)] =[x_{ih}(a),x_{hi}(bc)].$$ In particular, $[x_{ij}(a),x_{ji}(c)]$ is independent of $j\ne i$. It commutes with both defining roots, has square $1$, and has matrix image $I+acE_{ii}$.
  - Explanation: Controls opposite-root commutators when the reverse coefficient product vanishes.
- **C16** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-long-commutator)): For each coordinate $i$ and every nonzero word class $w$ satisfying $\|w\|\ge M$ and $\sigma(w)\ne\tau(w)$, there is an element $H_i(w)\in\mathop{\mathrm{St}}(R)$ with the following property. If $p,q$ are nonzero words with $$\|p\|,\|q\|\ge N,\qquad \|p\|+\|q\|\ge M, \qquad \sigma(p)\ne\tau(q),$$ then, for every $j\ne i$, $$\label{eq:long-opposite} [x_{ij}(p),x_{ji}(q)] =\begin{cases} H_i(w),& pq\text{ represents the nonzero class }w,\\ 1,& pq=0\text{ in }R. \end{cases}$$ The element $H_i(w)$ has square $1$ and matrix image $I+wE_{ii}$.
  - Explanation: Defines long-word opposite commutators independently of cuts and word replacements.
- **C17** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-wrong-row)): The subgroup $U(S,P)$ is abelian of exponent at most $2$, and the matrix map is injective on it. Every root with a consistent entry normalizes $U(S,P)$.
  - Explanation: Proves faithfulness and normalization of the elementary abelian wrong-row subgroup.
- **C18** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-relocation)): Suppose $z\in J(R)$ is represented by a finite word $$\label{eq:high-word} z=\prod_{\nu=1}^m x_{i_\nu j_\nu}(w_\nu), \qquad \|w_\nu\|\ge M,$$ where every $w_\nu$ is a nonzero word class. Label new coordinates by $(i,s)$, with $s\in\mathbb Z/L$. In the stable group, $$\label{eq:relocation} z=\prod_{\nu=1}^m x_{(i_\nu,\sigma(w_\nu)),(j_\nu,\tau(w_\nu))}(w_\nu).$$
  - Explanation: Relocates long-entry central representatives to coordinates carrying their word phases.
- **C19** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-odd-power)): Under the hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#lem:relocation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:relocation">18</a>, $$\label{eq:odd-power} W_z(C_L)=z^L.$$
  - Explanation: Identifies cyclic evaluation of a long-entry central representative with its odd-modulus power.
- **C20** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-high-entries)): For every integer $M\ge1$, each element of $$\ker\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{St}}(R)\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{St}}(R/R_{\ge2M})\bigr)$$ can be represented by a finite product of roots whose entries are nonzero word classes of lengths at least $M$.
  - Explanation: Expands elements invisible in a degree truncation into roots with long word entries.
- **C21** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-detection)): Degree truncations detect the stable Steinberg group: $$\label{eq:stable-detection} \bigcap_{d\ge1} \ker\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{St}}(R)\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{St}}(R/R_{\ge d})\bigr)=1.$$
  - Explanation: Proves that the intersection of all stable degree-truncation kernels is trivial.
- **C22** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-finite-rank-input)): For such an algebra $B$, the following statements hold.* 1. *Every tuple of columns of $B^m$ with linearly independent reductions in $\mathbb F_2^m$ extends to an invertible matrix.* 2. *The kernel $J_r(B)$ is central in $\mathop{\mathrm{St}}_r(B)$ for $r\geq6$.* 3. *For $r\geq5$, the group $\mathop{\mathrm{E}}_r(B)$ is normal in $\mathop{\mathrm{GL}}_r(B)$, and the natural maps $$\mathop{\mathrm{GL}}_r(B)/\mathop{\mathrm{E}}_r(B)\longrightarrow \mathop{\mathrm{GL}}_{r+1}(B)/\mathop{\mathrm{E}}_{r+1}(B)$$ are isomorphisms.
  - Explanation: States companion inputs on column completion, finite-rank centrality, and elementary quotient stabilization.
- **C23** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-stability)): Let $B=\mathbb F_2\oplus I_B$ satisfy the hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#prop:finite-rank-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:finite-rank-input">22</a>. For every $n\geq12$, the standard stabilization map $J_n(B)\to J_{n+1}(B)$ is an isomorphism, and the natural homomorphism $$\mathop{\mathrm{St}}_n(B)\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{St}}(B)$$ is injective.
  - Explanation: Proves stabilization of the central kernel and injectivity from fixed rank into the stable group.
- **C24** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-stabilizer-uce)): The map $\widetilde S_{r,l}\to S_{r,l}$ is a universal central extension. Consequently the inclusion of the complementary block induces a natural isomorphism $$\label{eq:stabilizer-homology} H_2(\mathop{\mathrm{E}}_r(B);\mathbb Z)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ } H_2(S_{r,l};\mathbb Z),$$ and both groups identify naturally with $J_r(B)$.
  - Explanation: Identifies frame-stabilizer second integral homology through a universal central extension.
- **C25** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-ordered-frames)): The augmented chain complex $C_\bullet(m)$ is exact in degrees at most $m-2$.
  - Explanation: Proves exactness of the augmented ordered-frame complex in the stated range.
- **C26** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-finite-quotients)): For every $d\ge1$, the group $\mathop{\mathrm{St}}_{12}(R/R_{\ge d})$ is finite. The homomorphisms $$\mathop{\mathrm{St}}_{12}(R)\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{St}}_{12}(R/R_{\ge d}),\qquad d\ge1,$$ separate the elements of $\mathop{\mathrm{St}}_{12}(R)$.
  - Explanation: Proves finiteness of the fixed-rank degree quotients and separation of group elements.

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-ring-input",
        "exact_quote": "There is an infinite finitely presented unital associative algebra $$R=\\bigoplus_{d\\geq0}R_d=\\mathbb F_2\\oplus I,\n \\qquad R_0=\\mathbb F_2,\n \\qquad I=\\bigoplus_{d\\geq1}R_d,$$ such that every $R_d$ is finite-dimensional and $I$ is matrix-nil. This algebra has the word presentation and hierarchy specified in Proposition <a href=\"#prop:hierarchy-input\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:hierarchy-input\">3</a>.",
        "source_id": "PM-247.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-prop-ring-input",

This algebra has the word presentation and hierarchy specified in Proposition <a href=\"#prop:hierarchy-input\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:hierarchy-input\">3</a>.",
          "source_id": "PM-247.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "There is an infinite finitely presented unital associative algebra $$R=\\bigoplus_{d\\geq0}R_d=\\mathbb F_2\\oplus I,\n \\qquad R_0=\\mathbb F_2,\n \\qquad I=\\bigoplus_{d\\geq1}R_d,$$ such that every $R_d$ is finite-dimensional and $I$ is matrix-nil. This algebra has the word presentation and hierarchy specified in Proposition <a href=\"#prop:hierarchy-input\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:hierarchy-input\">3</a>.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-hierarchy-input",
        "exact_quote": "The algebra $R$ in Proposition <a href=\"#prop:ring-input\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:ring-input\">2</a> is the first algebra $A_0$ of a sequence $(A_j)_{j\\geq0}$ with the following properties. There are positive integers $c,k,h_0,D$, with $D>10(c+1)(k+1)$, and $$h_j=h_0D^j,\\qquad L_j=2\\cdot2^{h_j}+1.$$ For each $j\\geq0$:*\n\n1.  *$A_j$ is presented by a finite alphabet $\\Sigma_j$, finitely many replacements, and finitely many zero declarations, all of length at most $k$. It is graded by word length.*\n\n2.  *There is a substitution $E_j$ sending each letter of $\\Sigma_{j+1}$ to a word of length $L_j$ over $\\Sigma_j$. Extended by concatenation, it induces a unital algebra homomorphism $A_{j+1}\\to A_j$.*\n\n\n\n3.  *A specified subset of $\\Sigma_j$ gives cuts immediately before its letters. There are integers $B_j,T_j\\geq0$ and a finite set of words $\\mathcal T_j$ such that every nonzero word $w$ of length at least $T_j$ has the unique decomposition $$\\label{eq:token-decomposition}\n     w=p_w\\,t_1\\cdots t_r\\,s_w,\n     \\qquad |p_w|,|s_w|\\leq B_j,\\qquad t_i\\in\\mathcal T_j.$$ Here $p_w$ precedes the first cut, $s_w$ begins at the last cut, and the $t_i$ are exactly the intervals between consecutive cuts. A cut is counted only if its following letter occurs in the word. For each $t\\in\\mathcal T_j$ there is a nonempty word $u_t$ over $\\Sigma_{j+1}$ such that $$\\label{eq:token-upper-word}\n     1\\leq |u_t|\\leq k,\\qquad\n     |t|=L_j|u_t|,\\qquad [t]=[E_j(u_t)]\\quad\\hbox{in }A_j.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-247.1"
      },

It is graded by word length.*\n\n2.  *There is a substitution $E_j$ sending each letter of $\\Sigma_{j+1}$ to a word of length $L_j$ over $\\Sigma_j$.     {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-phases",
        "exact_quote": "For every integer $d\\geq0$, put $$L=L_0L_1\\cdots L_d.$$ There is an integer $N\\geq1$ such that every nonzero word $w$ in $R$ of length at least $N$ has a start phase $\\sigma(w)\\in\\mathbb Z/L\\mathbb Z$ and an end phase $$\\tau(w)=\\sigma(w)+|w|\\in\\mathbb Z/L\\mathbb Z$$ with the following properties:*\n\n1.  *Both phases depend only on the basis class of $w$.*\n\n\n2.  *If $v$ is a contiguous subword of $w$, has length at least $N$, and starts at offset $b$ from the beginning of $w$, then $$\\label{eq:phase-subword}\n     \\sigma(v)=\\sigma(w)+b.$$*\n\n*The integers $L$ so obtained are odd and arbitrarily large.",
        "source_id": "PM-247.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-phases",
          "exact_quote": "For every integer $d\\geq0$, put $$L=L_0L_1\\cdots L_d.$$ There is an integer $N\\geq1$ such that every nonzero word $w$ in $R$ of length at least $N$ has a start phase $\\sigma(w)\\in\\mathbb Z/L\\mathbb Z$ and an end phase $$\\tau(w)=\\sigma(w)+|w|\\in\\mathbb Z/L\\mathbb Z$$ with the following properties:*\n\n1.  *Both phases depend only on the basis class of $w$.*\n\n\n2.  *If $v$ is a contiguous subword of $w$, has length at least $N$, and starts at offset $b$ from the beginning of $w$, then $$\\label{eq:phase-subword}\n     \\sigma(v)=\\sigma(w)+b.$$*\n\n*The integers $L$ so obtained are odd and arbitrarily large.",
          "source_id": "PM-247.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "For every integer $d\\geq0$, put $$L=L_0L_1\\cdots L_d.$$ There is an integer $N\\geq1$ such that every nonzero word $w$ in $R$ of length at least $N$ has a start phase $\\sigma(w)\\in\\mathbb Z/L\\mathbb Z$ and an end phase $$\\tau(w)=\\sigma(w)+|w|\\in\\mathbb Z/L\\mathbb Z$$ with the following properties:*\n\n1.  *Both phases depend only on the basis class of $w$.*\n\n\n2.  *If $v$ is a contiguous subword of $w$, has length at least $N$, and starts at offset $b$ from the beginning of $w$, then $$\\label{eq:phase-subword}\n     \\sigma(v)=\\sigma(w)+b.$$*\n\n*The integers $L$ so obtained are odd and arbitrarily large.",
      "explanation": "Constructs class-invariant start and end phases with the subword offset law and unbounded odd moduli.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-cor-phase-products",
        "exact_quote": "Fix $L,N,\\sigma,\\tau$ from Lemma <a href=\"#lem:phases\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:phases\">4</a>. If $u,v$ are nonzero basis words of lengths at least $N$, then $$\\tau(u)\\ne\\sigma(v)\\quad\\Longrightarrow\\quad uv=0.$$ If $uv\\ne0$, its phases satisfy $\\sigma(uv)=\\sigma(u)$ and $\\tau(uv)=\\tau(v)$.",
        "source_id": "PM-247.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-cor-phase-products",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-strip",
        "exact_quote": "Let $S,T$ be disjoint finite sets of coordinates. The subgroup generated by $x_{ij}(a)$ with $i\\in S$, $j\\in T$ maps isomorphically onto the additive group of matrices supported in $S\\times T$ under $u\\mapsto\n\\pi(u)-I$. A word supported on $S$ normalizes this subgroup and acts on its entries by left multiplication by its matrix on $S$. On the reverse strip $T\\times S$, its action is right multiplication by the inverse matrix on $S$.",
        "source_id": "PM-247.1"
      },
      "evidence": [

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-permutations",
        "exact_quote": "In characteristic two, every permutation of a finite set of coordinates is induced on roots by conjugation in the Steinberg group after adjoining an outside coordinate if necessary. In particular, relabeling the finitely many coordinates in a representative of $z\\in J(B)$ leaves the element $z$ unchanged in $\\mathop{\\mathrm{St}}(B)$.",
        "source_id": "PM-247.1"
      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-commutator",
        "exact_quote": "In any group, let $A=[X,Y]$ and $B'=[Y,Z]$. If $$[X,Z]=[A,X]=[B',Z]=[A,B']=1,$$ then $[A,Z]=[X,B']$. This common commutator commutes with $A,Z,X,B'$.",
        "source_id": "PM-247.1"
      },
      "evidence": [

          "source_id": "PM-247.1"
        }
      ],

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-block-laws",
        "exact_quote": "The block roots satisfy the Steinberg relations over $\\mathop{\\mathrm{Mat}}_b(B)$: $$\\begin{aligned}\n X_{ij}(A+A')&=X_{ij}(A)X_{ij}(A'),\\\\\n [X_{ij}(A),X_{kl}(A')]&=1\n       &&\\text{if }j\\ne k\\text{ and }i\\ne l,\\\\\n [X_{ij}(A),X_{jk}(A')]&=X_{ik}(AA')\n       &&\\text{if }i,j,k\\text{ are distinct}.\n\\end{aligned}$$ Consequently they define a homomorphism $\\mathop{\\mathrm{St}}_n(\\mathop{\\mathrm{Mat}}_b(B))\\longrightarrow\\mathop{\\mathrm{St}}_{nb}(B)$. Moreover, for every $P\\in\\mathop{\\mathrm{GL}}_b(\\mathbb F_2)$ there is an element $q\\in\\mathop{\\mathrm{St}}_{nb}(B)$ such that $$q^{-1}X_{ij}(A)q=X_{ij}(P^{-1}AP)\n \\qquad(i\\ne j).$$ Its matrix image is the block diagonal matrix with a copy of $P$ in every outer block.",
        "source_id": "PM-247.1"
      },

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-triangular",
        "exact_quote": "Let $U$ be generated by the roots $x_{(i,s),(j,t)}(a)$ with $s<t$, allowing $i=j$.         "anchor": "stmt-lem-long-commutator",
        "exact_quote": "For each coordinate $i$ and every nonzero word class $w$ satisfying $|w|\\ge M$ and $\\sigma(w)\\ne\\tau(w)$, there is an element $H_i(w)\\in\\mathop{\\mathrm{St}}(R)$ with the following property. If $p,q$ are nonzero words with $$|p|,|q|\\ge N,\\qquad |p|+|q|\\ge M,\n \\qquad \\sigma(p)\\ne\\tau(q),$$ then, for every $j\\ne i$, $$\\label{eq:long-opposite}\n [x_{ij}(p),x_{ji}(q)]\n =\\begin{cases}\n    H_i(w),& pq\\text{ represents the nonzero class }w,\\\\\n    1,& pq=0\\text{ in }R.\n   \\end{cases}$$ The element $H_i(w)$ has square $1$ and matrix image $I+wE_{ii}$.",

      },

          "source_id": "PM-247.1"

      "exact_statement": "For each coordinate $i$ and every nonzero word class $w$ satisfying $|w|\\ge M$ and $\\sigma(w)\\ne\\tau(w)$, there is an element $H_i(w)\\in\\mathop{\\mathrm{St}}(R)$ with the following property.     {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-finite-rank-input",
        "exact_quote": "For such an algebra $B$, the following statements hold.*\n\n1.  *Every tuple of columns of $B^m$ with linearly independent reductions in $\\mathbb F_2^m$ extends to an invertible matrix.*\n\n2.  *The kernel $J_r(B)$ is central in $\\mathop{\\mathrm{St}}_r(B)$ for $r\\geq6$.*\n\n3.  *For $r\\geq5$, the group $\\mathop{\\mathrm{E}}_r(B)$ is normal in $\\mathop{\\mathrm{GL}}_r(B)$, and the natural maps $$\\mathop{\\mathrm{GL}}_r(B)/\\mathop{\\mathrm{E}}_r(B)\\longrightarrow\n     \\mathop{\\mathrm{GL}}_{r+1}(B)/\\mathop{\\mathrm{E}}_{r+1}(B)$$ are isomorphisms.",
        "source_id": "PM-247.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-prop-finite-rank-input",
          "exact_quote": "For such an algebra $B$, the following statements hold.*\n\n1.  *Every tuple of columns of $B^m$ with linearly independent reductions in $\\mathbb F_2^m$ extends to an invertible matrix.*\n\n2.  *The kernel $J_r(B)$ is central in $\\mathop{\\mathrm{St}}_r(B)$ for $r\\geq6$.*\n\n3.  *For $r\\geq5$, the group $\\mathop{\\mathrm{E}}_r(B)$ is normal in $\\mathop{\\mathrm{GL}}_r(B)$, and the natural maps $$\\mathop{\\mathrm{GL}}_r(B)/\\mathop{\\mathrm{E}}_r(B)\\longrightarrow\n     \\mathop{\\mathrm{GL}}_{r+1}(B)/\\mathop{\\mathrm{E}}_{r+1}(B)$$ are isomorphisms.",
          "source_id": "PM-247.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "For such an algebra $B$, the following statements hold.*\n\n1.  *Every tuple of columns of $B^m$ with linearly independent reductions in $\\mathbb F_2^m$ extends to an invertible matrix.*\n\n2.  *The kernel $J_r(B)$ is central in $\\mathop{\\mathrm{St}}_r(B)$ for $r\\geq6$.*\n\n3.  *For $r\\geq5$, the group $\\mathop{\\mathrm{E}}_r(B)$ is normal in $\\mathop{\\mathrm{GL}}_r(B)$, and the natural maps $$\\mathop{\\mathrm{GL}}_r(B)/\\mathop{\\mathrm{E}}_r(B)\\longrightarrow\n     \\mathop{\\mathrm{GL}}_{r+1}(B)/\\mathop{\\mathrm{E}}_{r+1}(B)$$ are isomorphisms.",
      "explanation": "States companion inputs on column completion, finite-rank centrality, and elementary quotient stabilization.",

      "hypotheses": [

          "anchor": "stmt-prop-finite-rank-input",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **periodic group** ([source](#sec:introduction)): A group is *periodic* if each of its elements has finite order.
- **residually finite group** ([source](#sec:introduction)): It is *residually finite* if, for every nonidentity element $g$, there is a homomorphism to a finite group whose value at $g$ is nonidentity.
- **2-group** ([source](#sec:introduction)): We use *2-group* to mean a group in which every element has finite 2-power order; the group itself need not be finite.
- **ordinary finite presentation** ([source](#sec:introduction)): An *ordinary finite presentation* is a quotient of a free group on finitely many generators by the normal closure of finitely many words.
- **matrix-nil ideal** ([source](#sec:ring-phases)): An ideal $I$ of a unital associative algebra is *matrix-nil* if every square matrix of every finite size with entries in $I$ is nilpotent.
- **replacement** ([source](#sec:ring-phases)): In a presentation by words, a *replacement* is a relation $v=v'$ between nonempty words of equal length, usable in either direction inside any word.
- **zero declaration** ([source](#sec:ring-phases)): A *zero declaration* is a relation $v=0$ for a nonempty word $v$.
- **commutator convention** ([source](#sec:steinberg)): Our convention is $[X,Y]=XYX^{-1}Y^{-1}$.
- **elementary matrix** ([source](#sec:steinberg)): The elementary matrix $e_{ij}(a)$ is the identity with $a$ added in position $(i,j)$.
- **finite-rank matrix kernel** ([source](#sec:steinberg)): Write $J_n(B)=\ker\pi_n$.
- **ordered frame** ([source](#pm-h-17)): An *ordered frame* of $B^m$ is an ordered tuple of columns whose reductions modulo $I_B$ are linearly independent.
- **perfect group** ([source](#pm-h-16)): A group is *perfect* if it equals its commutator subgroup.

            "exact_quote": "The algebra $R$ in Proposition <a href=\"#prop:ring-input\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:ring-input\">2</a> is the first algebra $A_0$ of a sequence $(A_j)_{j\\geq0}$ with the following properties. There are positive integers $c,k,h_0,D$, with $D>10(c+1)(k+1)$, and $$h_j=h_0D^j,\\qquad L_j=2\\cdot2^{h_j}+1.$$ For each $j\\geq0$:*\n\n1.  *$A_j$ is presented by a finite alphabet $\\Sigma_j$, finitely many replacements, and finitely many zero declarations, all of length at most $k$. It is graded by word length.*\n\n2.  *There is a substitution $E_j$ sending each letter of $\\Sigma_{j+1}$ to a word of length $L_j$ over $\\Sigma_j$. Extended by concatenation, it induces a unital algebra homomorphism $A_{j+1}\\to A_j$.*\n\n\n\n3.  *A specified subset of $\\Sigma_j$ gives cuts immediately before its letters. There are integers $B_j,T_j\\geq0$ and a finite set of words $\\mathcal T_j$ such that every nonzero word $w$ of length at least $T_j$ has the unique decomposition $$\\label{eq:token-decomposition}\n     w=p_w\\,t_1\\cdots t_r\\,s_w,\n     \\qquad |p_w|,|s_w|\\leq B_j,\\qquad t_i\\in\\mathcal T_j.$$ Here $p_w$ precedes the first cut, $s_w$ begins at the last cut, and the $t_i$ are exactly the intervals between consecutive cuts. A cut is counted only if its following letter occurs in the word. For each $t\\in\\mathcal T_j$ there is a nonempty word $u_t$ over $\\Sigma_{j+1}$ such that $$\\label{eq:token-upper-word}\n     1\\leq |u_t|\\leq k,\\qquad\n     |t|=L_j|u_t|,\\qquad [t]=[E_j(u_t)]\\quad\\hbox{in }A_j.$$",

          }

There are positive integers $c,k,h_0,D$, with $D>10(c+1)(k+1)$, and $$h_j=h_0D^j,\\qquad L_j=2\\cdot2^{h_j}+1.$$ For each $j\\geq0$:*\n\n1.  *$A_j$ is presented by a finite alphabet $\\Sigma_j$, finitely many replacements, and finitely many zero declarations, all of length at most $k$. It is graded by word length.*\n\n2.  *There is a substitution $E_j$ sending each letter of $\\Sigma_{j+1}$ to a word of length $L_j$ over $\\Sigma_j$. Extended by concatenation, it induces a unital algebra homomorphism $A_{j+1}\\to A_j$.*\n\n\n\n3.  *A specified subset of $\\Sigma_j$ gives cuts immediately before its letters. There are integers $B_j,T_j\\geq0$ and a finite set of words $\\mathcal T_j$ such that every nonzero word $w$ of length at least $T_j$ has the unique decomposition $$\\label{eq:token-decomposition}\n     w=p_w\\,t_1\\cdots t_r\\,s_w,\n     \\qquad |p_w|,|s_w|\\leq B_j,\\qquad t_i\\in\\mathcal T_j.$$ Here $p_w$ precedes the first cut, $s_w$ begins at the last cut, and the $t_i$ are exactly the intervals between consecutive cuts. A cut is counted only if its following letter occurs in the word. For each $t\\in\\mathcal T_j$ there is a nonempty word $u_t$ over $\\Sigma_{j+1}$ such that $$\\label{eq:token-upper-word}\n     1\\leq |u_t|\\leq k,\\qquad\n     |t|=L_j|u_t|,\\qquad [t]=[E_j(u_t)]\\quad\\hbox{in }A_j.$$",
            "source_id": "PM-247.1"
          }
        ]
      },
      "relation": "=",
      "value_latex": "2\\cdot2^{h_j}+1"

        "hypotheses": [
          {
            "anchor": "stmt-prop-finite-rank-input",
            "exact_quote": "For such an algebra $B$, the following statements hold.*\n\n1.  *Every tuple of columns of $B^m$ with linearly independent reductions in $\\mathbb F_2^m$ extends to an invertible matrix.*\n\n2.  *The kernel $J_r(B)$ is central in $\\mathop{\\mathrm{St}}_r(B)$ for $r\\geq6$.*\n\n3.  *For $r\\geq5$, the group $\\mathop{\\mathrm{E}}_r(B)$ is normal in $\\mathop{\\mathrm{GL}}_r(B)$, and the natural maps $$\\mathop{\\mathrm{GL}}_r(B)/\\mathop{\\mathrm{E}}_r(B)\\longrightarrow\n     \\mathop{\\mathrm{GL}}_{r+1}(B)/\\mathop{\\mathrm{E}}_{r+1}(B)$$ are isomorphisms.",
            "source_id": "PM-247.1"
          }
        ],

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R1** [claim](#stmt-lem-phases) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-phases): Backward token induction followed by invariance under local replacements. -> Class-invariant phases with the subword law.
- **R2** [claim](#stmt-lem-stable-center) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-stable-center): Use faithful strips and an outside coordinate. -> Central stable matrix kernel.
- **R3** [claim](#stmt-prop-relative-torsion) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-relative-torsion): Triangularize cyclic shifts of power-of-two dimension and combine evaluation with vanishing. -> Relative central torsion of finite power-of-two order.
- **R4** [claim](#stmt-lem-wrong-row) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-wrong-row): Control opposite roots and read collected coefficients in the word basis. -> Faithful wrong-row subgroup.
- **R5** [claim](#stmt-lem-relocation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-relocation): Change rank-one coefficient maps and discard faithful-strip corrections. -> Central representatives with phase-labeled coordinates.
- **R6** [claim](#stmt-prop-odd-power) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-odd-power): Collect commuting cyclic-evaluation factors by phase offset. -> Odd-power identity for cyclic evaluation.
- **R7** [claim](#stmt-thm-detection) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thm-detection): Choose the odd modulus before the long representative and combine coprime annihilating powers. -> Trivial intersection of stable truncation kernels.

- **R10** [claim](#stmt-prop-finite-quotients) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-finite-quotients): Combine Schur finiteness with the natural stabilization square. -> Finite truncation quotients separating group elements.
- **R11** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-finite-quotients-2): Take the degree-zero kernel and combine matrix nilpotence with relative central torsion. -> Infinite ordinarily finitely presented residually finite group with power-of-two element orders.

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **I1**: A cyclic shift of power-of-two size has nilpotent difference from the identity in characteristic two.
- **I2**: Mismatched end and start phases force the product of sufficiently long nonzero basis words to vanish.
- **I3**: Disjoint coordinate strips are faithfully detected by their matrix entries.
- **I4**: Removing the wrap preserves the evaluated Steinberg element under the stated size bound and identity-matrix hypothesis.
- **I5**: Fixed-rank stabilization is injective under the matrix-nil hypotheses and the stated rank bound.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **A1**: Infer a Steinberg identity solely from an identity of elementary matrices. -> The matrix map has a central kernel that must also be controlled.
- **A2**: Apply cyclic-shift nilpotence to an arbitrary shift size. -> The triangularization argument depends on power-of-two size.
- **A3**: Treat residual finiteness as a uniform bound on element orders. -> The constructed group has unbounded element orders.
- **A4**: Read separation by finite quotients as an effective degree bound for every input word. -> The argument chooses a detecting truncation without supplying such an input-dependent bound.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The numerical cyclic-shift identity in the relative-torsion proof admits finite exact computation. This calculation does not certify the infinite group construction or the central-kernel identities.

```python
# INPUTS: integer b on stdin
# PRECONDITIONS: 1 <= b <= 12; arithmetic reduced modulo 2
# OUTPUTS: JSON matrix (C_b-I)^b over F_2
# TIME: O(b^3 log b) integer matrix operations; intermediate integers grow
# SPACE: O(b^2) integer entries, with O(b) bits per entry
# ARITHMETIC: exact FLINT integer matrices followed by reduction modulo 2
# FAILURE: invalid or out-of-range input exits nonzero
import sys, json
from flint import fmpz_mat
b = int(sys.stdin.read())
assert 1 <= b <= 12
entries = [[int(j == (i + 1) % b) - int(i == j) for j in range(b)] for i in range(b)]
P = fmpz_mat(entries) ** b
print(json.dumps([[int(P[i,j]) % 2 for j in range(b)] for i in range(b)], separators=(',', ':')))
```

| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Block-root evaluations are algebraic matrix substitutions. A hardware architecture, execution schedule, and acceleration result are ABSENT. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Residual finiteness supplies separating finite images, but a security construction, adversarial model, and hardness assumption are ABSENT. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **L-discrete** (formal_scope): The existence theorem concerns abstract discrete groups and ordinary finite presentations. Blocked use: ['Interpreting the presentation as a presentation only in a variety of groups or in the category of pro-2 groups.']
- **L-exponent** (counterexample_scope): Element orders in the constructed group are unbounded. Blocked use: ['Using the theorem to obtain an infinite finitely generated residually finite group with a uniform bound on element orders.']
- **L-companion** (hypothesis): The construction requires the companion algebra and its finite local-rule hierarchy. Blocked use: ['Applying detection to an arbitrary graded algebra on the basis of matrix-nilness alone.']
- **L-matrix-kernel** (formal_scope): Equality of matrix images does not by itself settle equality of arbitrary Steinberg elements. Blocked use: ['Replacing detection of the central kernel by matrix multiplication alone.']
- **L-strips** (hypothesis): Faithful strips require disjoint finite source and target coordinate sets. Blocked use: ['Applying strip injectivity to overlapping coordinate sets.']
- **L-phases** (hypothesis): Phase-product vanishing is asserted for nonzero basis words meeting the phase length threshold. Blocked use: ['Extending the phase mismatch criterion to arbitrary short words without its hypotheses.']
- **L-wrap** (edge_case): Removing the wrap requires an identity matrix word and a shift dimension strictly beyond the stated bound. Blocked use: ['Treating the bound as non-strict or applying wrap removal to an arbitrary polynomial root word.']
- **L-relative** (hypothesis): The relative torsion conclusion requires membership in the stable central kernel and trivial reduction over the degree-zero field. Blocked use: ['Applying relative central-kernel torsion to an arbitrary Steinberg element.']
- **L-stability** (hypothesis): The stated fixed-rank stabilization theorem applies under the finite-rank input hypotheses and the stated rank threshold. Blocked use: ['Using this theorem to assert injectivity at smaller ranks or for algebras outside its hypotheses.']
- **L-quantifiers** (nonconstructive): Stable detection uses all degree truncations; the separating degree depends on the nonidentity element. Blocked use: ['Inferring that one fixed finite degree quotient detects every group element.', 'Claiming a uniform effective separating-degree bound from this proof.']
- **L-order-choice** (formal_scope): The odd phase modulus is chosen using a fixed representative before selecting a long-entry representative. Blocked use: ['Choosing the modulus only after changing to long entries and assuming the original vanishing threshold still controls the new choice.']
- **L-finite-check** (formal_scope): Finite cyclic-shift calculations check an auxiliary identity; they do not certify the infinite-group existence theorem. Blocked use: ['Treating finite matrix experiments as verification of the companion construction, stable central-kernel detection, or ordinary finite presentability.']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove that the infinite, ordinarily finitely presented periodic Steinberg group $`\Gamma=\mathop{\mathrm{St}}\nolimits _{12}(R)`$ of a companion paper is residually finite. Its finite-index subgroup $`G=\ker(\Gamma\to\mathop{\mathrm{St}}\nolimits _{12}(\mathbb F_2))`$ is infinite, ordinarily finitely presented, and residually finite, and every element of <i>G</i> has finite 2-power order. The orders of its elements are unbounded. Phases of long words in the companion's graded algebra allow finite degree truncations to detect all elements of Γ, including the central kernel.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

A group is *periodic* if each of its elements has finite order. It is *residually finite* if, for every nonidentity element $g$, there is a homomorphism to a finite group whose value at $g$ is nonidentity. We use *2-group* to mean a group in which every element has finite 2-power order; the group itself need not be finite. An *ordinary finite presentation* is a quotient of a free group on finitely many generators by the normal closure of finitely many words. We prove the following.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There exists an infinite residually finite group with an ordinary finite presentation in which every element has finite 2-power order.*

</div>

The group is an abstract discrete group. Finite presentation is meant in that category, rather than in a variety of groups or in the category of pro-2 groups. The orders of its elements are necessarily unbounded.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### History and relation to earlier constructions

Burnside’s finiteness questions <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Burnside1902](#ref-Burnside1902)] ask how finite-order conditions constrain a group with finitely many generators. Golod’s construction <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Golod1964](#ref-Golod1964), Example 2], based on the Golod–Shafarevich dimension argument <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[GolodShafarevich1964](#ref-GolodShafarevich1964)], already produces infinite finitely generated residually finite $p$-groups. Its algebraic mechanism is particularly relevant here: nilpotence gives finite $p$-power orders, while a grading separates elements in finite quotients. Grigorchuk later gave a concrete infinite finitely generated residually finite 2-group <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Grigorchuk1980](#ref-Grigorchuk1980)]; his original theorem also establishes that this group is not finitely presented. Thus residual finiteness and fixed-prime torsion can coexist with finite generation. The additional requirement in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> is an ordinary finite presentation.

The question whether an infinite periodic group can have an ordinary finite presentation is recorded in <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[OlshanskiiSapir2003](#ref-OlshanskiiSapir2003), p. 45] and again in <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[HaettelOsajda2025](#ref-HaettelOsajda2025), Section 2.2(IX)]. The torsion-by-cyclic groups of <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[OlshanskiiSapir2003](#ref-OlshanskiiSapir2003), Theorem 1.1] have an infinite cyclic quotient. They establish a different finite-presentation result and are not themselves periodic. The companion paper <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Companion](#ref-Companion)] constructs an infinite finitely presented periodic group using a graded algebra with a matrix-nil positive-degree ideal. The present paper strengthens that construction by proving residual finiteness and passing to a finite-index subgroup with 2-power torsion. The companion’s algebra and finite local-rule hierarchy are essential inputs; we state their precise properties in Section <a href="#sec:ring-phases" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:ring-phases">2</a>.

A bound on all element orders would change the problem. Novikov and Adian constructed infinite finitely generated groups of bounded exponent <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[NovikovAdian1968](#ref-NovikovAdian1968)]. In the residually finite setting, however, Zel’manov’s solution of the restricted Burnside problem for 2-groups <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Zelmanov1992](#ref-Zelmanov1992), Theorem 2] implies that a finitely generated residually finite group of exponent dividing $2^a$ is finite. Consequently the group in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> has unbounded exponent. Its finite quotients are nevertheless all 2-groups: a finite image has only elements of 2-power order, and Cauchy’s theorem excludes every odd prime divisor of its order.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The proof and its main ingredients

The companion supplies an infinite finitely presented graded algebra $$R=\mathbb F_2\oplus I,\qquad I=\bigoplus_{d\ge1}R_d,$$ such that every matrix in $M_n(I)$ is nilpotent, for every finite $n$. Each homogeneous component $R_d$ is finite-dimensional. The elementary matrix group $\mathop{\mathrm{E}}_n(R)$ is generated by the matrices $e_{ij}(a)$ obtained by adding $a\in R$ to an off-diagonal position of the identity. The Steinberg group $\mathop{\mathrm{St}}_n(R)$ is generated by symbols $x_{ij}(a)$ subject to the elementary addition and nonopposite-root commutator relations; these are written explicitly in Section <a href="#sec:steinberg" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:steinberg">3</a>. There is a natural map $$\mathop{\mathrm{St}}_n(R)\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{E}}_n(R),\qquad
 x_{ij}(a)\longmapsto e_{ij}(a).$$ Finite presentability of Steinberg groups in rank at least four over finitely presented unital rings is due to Krstić and McCool <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[KrsticMcCool](#ref-KrsticMcCool), Theorem 3]; an adapted proof is also given in <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Companion](#ref-Companion), Theorem 4.1]. We take $n=12$ and eventually use the kernel of reduction from $\mathop{\mathrm{St}}_{12}(R)$ to $\mathop{\mathrm{St}}_{12}(\mathbb F_2)$.

The degree truncations $R/R_{\ge d}$, where $R_{\ge d}=\bigoplus_{b\ge d}R_b$, are finite rings. They immediately detect nonidentity matrices over $R$. The main difficulty is to detect elements of the Steinberg kernel as well. We first work in the stable group $\mathop{\mathrm{St}}(R)=\varinjlim_n\mathop{\mathrm{St}}_n(R)$, put $\mathop{\mathrm{E}}(R)=\varinjlim_n\mathop{\mathrm{E}}_n(R)$, and write $$J(R)=\ker\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{St}}(R)\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{E}}(R)\bigr).$$ This kernel is central. We first prove detection in the stable group, where a finite calculation may always use additional coordinates. We return to fixed rank afterward.

Two calculations of one cyclic substitution provide the detection argument. If $z\in J(R)$, the grading replaces each homogeneous root entry $a\in R_d$ by $at^d$. Substituting the cyclic shift matrix $C_L$ for $t$ and expanding into ordinary roots gives an element $W_z(C_L)\in J(R)$. This is a matrix realization of a graded transfer. Weibel’s work on graded rings <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Weibel1987](#ref-Weibel1987)] develops the corresponding transfer operations and their eventual vanishing; it also implies $p$-primary torsion in relative graded $K$-groups in characteristic $p$. Following the elementary Steinberg treatment in <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Companion](#ref-Companion)], Section <a href="#sec:shifts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:shifts">4</a> proves directly that $$W_z(C_L)=1\quad\text{for all sufficiently large }L$$ whenever $z$ reduces to $1$ over $\mathbb F_2$. Taking $L$ to be a power of two also shows that every such $z$ has 2-power order.

The second calculation uses additional structure of this particular ring. Its finite local rules give a basis of nonzero word classes. For arbitrarily large odd integers $L$, each sufficiently long nonzero word has a start phase $\sigma(w)\in\mathbb Z/L$ and an end phase $\tau(w)=\sigma(w)+|w|$. These phases depend only on the word class, and a nonzero product must match end phase to start phase. Section <a href="#sec:ring-phases" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:ring-phases">2</a> derives the phases from the hierarchy’s bounded token decompositions.

If $z$ is missed by all degree truncations, it can be represented using roots whose entries are arbitrarily long words. The phases allow us to move the endpoints of each such root to coordinates labeled by its start and end phases, without changing $z$. Establishing this identity requires control of opposite-root commutators in the Steinberg group, as well as a subgroup on which the matrix map is injective. Sections <a href="#sec:long-entries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:long-entries">5</a> and <a href="#sec:detection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:detection">6</a> prove these facts and obtain, for the long-entry representative, $$W_z(C_L)=z^L.$$ We first choose an odd $L$ for which the first calculation vanishes, and then find a representative long enough for the second calculation. Thus $z^L=1$. Since $z$ also has 2-power order, it is the identity. This proves separation by finite degree truncations, the additional step beyond torsion supplied by graded transfers alone.

To pass back to twelve coordinates, we need the injectivity of $\mathop{\mathrm{St}}_{12}(R)\to\mathop{\mathrm{St}}(R)$. Stability over the local ring $R$ is covered by the classical theorem of Suslin and Tulenbaev <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[SuslinTulenbaev1976](#ref-SuslinTulenbaev1976)]. Section <a href="#sec:stability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:stability">7</a> gives a direct integral homology proof using ordered frames and universal central extensions of their stabilizers. This supplies the bridge from stable detection to the finitely presented group.

All arguments establishing these phase, stabilization, and detection claims are included below. We quote from the companion the algebra construction and the finite-rank elementary facts needed to apply them, with their hypotheses stated at the points of use. The final passage to finite groups and to the subgroup of 2-power torsion is carried out in Section <a href="#sec:construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:construction">8</a>.

<a id="sec:ring-phases"></a>
### The algebra and phases of long words

The ring used below has two relevant features. Its positive-degree matrices are nilpotent, which will give torsion, and its long nonzero words carry compatible positions modulo arbitrarily large odd integers, which will give detection by finite quotients. The first feature is an input from the companion paper. We derive the second from the word hierarchy in that paper.

An ideal $I$ of a unital associative algebra is *matrix-nil* if every square matrix of every finite size with entries in $I$ is nilpotent. All algebras in this paper are over $\mathbb F_2$.

<a id="stmt-prop-ring-input"></a>
<div id="prop:ring-input" class="proposition">

**Proposition 2** (The companion algebra). *There is an infinite finitely presented unital associative algebra $$R=\bigoplus_{d\geq0}R_d=\mathbb F_2\oplus I,
 \qquad R_0=\mathbb F_2,
 \qquad I=\bigoplus_{d\geq1}R_d,$$ such that every $R_d$ is finite-dimensional and $I$ is matrix-nil. This algebra has the word presentation and hierarchy specified in Proposition <a href="#prop:hierarchy-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:hierarchy-input">3</a>.*

</div>

This is the algebra constructed in <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Companion](#ref-Companion), Theorem 3.7]. We now give the precise part of its structure that we use. In a presentation by words, a *replacement* is a relation $v=v'$ between nonempty words of equal length, usable in either direction inside any word. A *zero declaration* is a relation $v=0$ for a nonempty word $v$. The length of a replacement is the common length of its two sides. For a word $w$, write $|w|$ for its length and $[w]$ for its image in the relevant algebra.

<a id="stmt-prop-hierarchy-input"></a>
<div id="prop:hierarchy-input" class="proposition">

**Proposition 3** (The companion hierarchy). *The algebra $R$ in Proposition <a href="#prop:ring-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:ring-input">2</a> is the first algebra $A_0$ of a sequence $(A_j)_{j\geq0}$ with the following properties. There are positive integers $c,k,h_0,D$, with $D>10(c+1)(k+1)$, and $$h_j=h_0D^j,\qquad L_j=2\cdot2^{h_j}+1.$$ For each $j\geq0$:*

1.  *$A_j$ is presented by a finite alphabet $\Sigma_j$, finitely many replacements, and finitely many zero declarations, all of length at most $k$. It is graded by word length.*

2.  *There is a substitution $E_j$ sending each letter of $\Sigma_{j+1}$ to a word of length $L_j$ over $\Sigma_j$. Extended by concatenation, it induces a unital algebra homomorphism $A_{j+1}\to A_j$.*

<a id="eq:token-decomposition"></a>
<a id="eq:token-upper-word"></a>
3.  *A specified subset of $\Sigma_j$ gives cuts immediately before its letters. There are integers $B_j,T_j\geq0$ and a finite set of words $\mathcal T_j$ such that every nonzero word $w$ of length at least $T_j$ has the unique decomposition $$\label{eq:token-decomposition}
     w=p_w\,t_1\cdots t_r\,s_w,
     \qquad |p_w|,|s_w|\leq B_j,\qquad t_i\in\mathcal T_j.$$ Here $p_w$ precedes the first cut, $s_w$ begins at the last cut, and the $t_i$ are exactly the intervals between consecutive cuts. A cut is counted only if its following letter occurs in the word. For each $t\in\mathcal T_j$ there is a nonempty word $u_t$ over $\Sigma_{j+1}$ such that $$\label{eq:token-upper-word}
     1\leq |u_t|\leq k,\qquad
     |t|=L_j|u_t|,\qquad [t]=[E_j(u_t)]\quad\hbox{in }A_j.$$*

</div>

These are the applicable conclusions of <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Companion](#ref-Companion), Proposition 2.2 and the choice of parameters in Section 3]. In particular, the cuts in (iii) are recognized from individual letters; restricting to a subword cannot create or move a cut. We will not need arbitrary concatenations of tokens to be nonzero.

<a id="eq:word-zero-criterion"></a>
We recall the elementary word-basis argument <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Companion](#ref-Companion), Lemma 2.1], since it will also identify exactly which equalities of long words must preserve their phases. Form a graph whose vertices are all words, including the empty word, and whose edges are replacements in arbitrary contexts. Quotienting the free algebra by the replacement relations leaves one basis vector for each connected component: the defining ideal is precisely the span of differences of words in a common component. Imposing the zero declarations then kills exactly the components containing a word with a zero-declared subword. Thus, in every $A_j$, the nonzero replacement classes form a basis, and $$\label{eq:word-zero-criterion}
 [w]=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \text{$w$ can be replaced by a word containing a zero-declared subword.}$$ In particular, a subword of a nonzero word is nonzero. The product of two basis classes is either zero or the class of their concatenation. We will often use the same symbol for a nonzero word and its basis class when it occurs as an algebra entry.

The finite alphabet also shows directly that each homogeneous part $R_d$ is finite-dimensional. Write $$R_{\geq d}=\bigoplus_{e\geq d}R_e.$$ For $d\geq1$ this is a two-sided ideal, and $R/R_{\geq d}$ is a finite ring. The remaining task in this Section is to extract compatible modular positions from the hierarchy.

<a id="stmt-lem-phases"></a>
<div id="lem:phases" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Phases of long words). *For every integer $d\geq0$, put $$L=L_0L_1\cdots L_d.$$ There is an integer $N\geq1$ such that every nonzero word $w$ in $R$ of length at least $N$ has a start phase $\sigma(w)\in\mathbb Z/L\mathbb Z$ and an end phase $$\tau(w)=\sigma(w)+|w|\in\mathbb Z/L\mathbb Z$$ with the following properties:*

1.  *Both phases depend only on the basis class of $w$.*

<a id="eq:phase-subword"></a>
2.  *If $v$ is a contiguous subword of $w$, has length at least $N$, and starts at offset $b$ from the beginning of $w$, then $$\label{eq:phase-subword}
     \sigma(v)=\sigma(w)+b.$$*

*The integers $L$ so obtained are odd and arbitrarily large.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-phases"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-phases</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $d$. First we construct phases for literal nonzero words, without claiming invariance under replacements. We work backward through the levels $j=d,d-1,\ldots,0$. At level $d+1$, use the trivial phase modulo the empty product $1$, with threshold $1$. Suppose that phases with the subword property have been constructed at level $j+1$ modulo $$L'=L_{j+1}\cdots L_d,$$ above a threshold $n_{j+1}\geq1$.

For every token $t\in\mathcal T_j$, choose one word $u_t$ satisfying <a href="#eq:token-upper-word" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:token-upper-word">[eq:token-upper-word]</a>, and use this same choice at every occurrence of $t$. For the decomposition <a href="#eq:token-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:token-decomposition">[eq:token-decomposition]</a>, set $$U(w)=u_{t_1}\cdots u_{t_r}.$$ Choose <a id="eq:literal-threshold"></a>$$\label{eq:literal-threshold}
 n_j\geq\max\{1,T_j,\,2B_j+L_j n_{j+1}\}.$$ Then every nonzero word $w$ of length at least $n_j$ has such a decomposition and $$|U(w)|=\frac{|w|-|p_w|-|s_w|}{L_j}\geq n_{j+1}.$$ Moreover, $U(w)$ is nonzero in $A_{j+1}$. Indeed, the substitution homomorphism and the token equalities give $$[w]=[p_w]\,[E_j(U(w))]\,[s_w],$$ so $[U(w)]=0$ would imply $[w]=0$. Define the literal start phase by <a id="eq:phase-induction"></a>$$\label{eq:phase-induction}
 \sigma_j(w)=L_j\sigma_{j+1}(U(w))-|p_w|
 \pmod{L_jL'}.$$ Multiplication by $L_j$ makes this independent of the representative chosen for the residue $\sigma_{j+1}(U(w))$ modulo $L'$.

<a id="eq:token-offset"></a>
We verify the subword property at level $j$. Let $v$ be a subword of $w$ of length at least $n_j$, starting at offset $b$. Its cuts are exactly the cuts of $w$ whose following letters lie in $v$. Consequently, its complete tokens are a consecutive subsequence of the complete tokens of $w$. Our fixed token choices make $U(v)$ a literal subword of $U(w)$. If $a$ is its upper-level offset, the lower-level distance to its first complete token can be computed in two ways: $$\label{eq:token-offset}
 b+|p_v|=|p_w|+L_j a.$$ Both upper words meet the upper-level threshold. Applying the inductive subword property and then <a href="#eq:token-offset" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:token-offset">[eq:token-offset]</a> yields $$\begin{aligned}
 \sigma_j(v)
 &=L_j\bigl(\sigma_{j+1}(U(w))+a\bigr)-|p_v|\\
 &=L_j\sigma_{j+1}(U(w))-|p_w|+b
 =\sigma_j(w)+b
 \pmod{L_jL'}.
\end{aligned}$$ This completes the backward induction.

At level zero we now have literal phases modulo $L$, with the subword property for lengths at least $n_0$. Only invariance under replacements remains. Choose $$N=2n_0+k.$$ Consider one replacement inside a nonzero word of length at least $N$. It affects at most $k$ letters, so an unchanged prefix or an unchanged suffix has length at least $n_0$. That common subword has the same offset before and after the replacement, because the replacement preserves length. Applying the literal subword property to both words shows that their phases agree. Every intermediate word in a nonzero replacement class is nonzero. Chaining these equalities proves that the start phase is constant on each class. Word length is constant on each class as well, so the end phase is also invariant. The subword property already proved continues to hold after increasing the threshold from $n_0$ to $N$. Finally, every $L_j$ is an odd integer greater than one, so the products $L_0\cdots L_d$ are odd and unbounded. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cor-phase-products"></a>
<div id="cor:phase-products" class="corollary">

**Corollary 5**. *Fix $L,N,\sigma,\tau$ from Lemma <a href="#lem:phases" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:phases">4</a>. If $u,v$ are nonzero basis words of lengths at least $N$, then $$\tau(u)\ne\sigma(v)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad uv=0.$$ If $uv\ne0$, its phases satisfy $\sigma(uv)=\sigma(u)$ and $\tau(uv)=\tau(v)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-phase-products"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-phase-products</summary>

*Proof.* If the concatenation is nonzero, apply <a href="#eq:phase-subword" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:phase-subword">[eq:phase-subword]</a> to its prefix $u$ and its suffix $v$. This gives $\sigma(uv)=\sigma(u)$ and $\sigma(v)=\sigma(uv)+|u|=\tau(u)$; adding $|v|$ gives the end-phase identity. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:steinberg"></a>
### Steinberg groups and faithful strips

We next introduce the group that records elementary matrix operations before imposing any relations beyond the Steinberg relations. Its kernel over matrices is the part that finite degree truncations must detect. All algebras in this section are unital associative $\mathbb F_2$-algebras.

<a id="eq:root-addition"></a>
<a id="eq:root-commuting"></a>
<a id="eq:root-product"></a>
For such an algebra $B$ and $n\ge3$, the group $\mathop{\mathrm{St}}_n(B)$ has generators $x_{ij}(a)$, where $a\in B$ and $1\le i,j\le n$ with $i\ne j$, and relations $$\begin{aligned}
 x_{ij}(a)x_{ij}(b)&=x_{ij}(a+b),\label{eq:root-addition}\\
 [x_{ij}(a),x_{lt}(b)]&=1
       &&(j\ne l,\ i\ne t),\label{eq:root-commuting}\\
 [x_{ij}(a),x_{jt}(b)]&=x_{it}(ab)
       &&(i,j,t\text{ distinct}).\label{eq:root-product}
\end{aligned}$$ Our convention is $[X,Y]=XYX^{-1}Y^{-1}$. In particular, $x_{ij}(0)=1$ and each root element has square $1$. The elementary matrix $e_{ij}(a)$ is the identity with $a$ added in position $(i,j)$. These matrices satisfy <a href="#eq:root-addition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:root-addition">[eq:root-addition]</a>–<a href="#eq:root-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:root-product">[eq:root-product]</a>, giving an epimorphism $$\pi_n:\mathop{\mathrm{St}}_n(B)\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{E}}_n(B)
       :=\langle e_{ij}(a):a\in B,\ i\ne j\rangle.$$ Write $J_n(B)=\ker\pi_n$. We use the same presentation with a countable index set to define $\mathop{\mathrm{St}}(B)$, and put $$\mathop{\mathrm{E}}(B)=\varinjlim_n\mathop{\mathrm{E}}_n(B),\qquad
 J(B)=\ker\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{St}}(B)\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{E}}(B)\bigr).$$ Thus $\mathop{\mathrm{St}}(B)=\varinjlim_n\mathop{\mathrm{St}}_n(B)$: each word and each finite derivation uses only finitely many indices. This is a direct limit of discrete groups. No completion is involved. The fixed-rank injectivity needed for $R$ will be proved separately in Section <a href="#sec:stability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:stability">7</a>.

The presentation and its relation to algebraic $K$-theory are classical; see <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Milnor](#ref-Milnor), [WeibelK](#ref-WeibelK)]. We will use the following elementary consequences repeatedly. They also explain when an identity of matrices does imply the corresponding identity in the Steinberg group.

<a id="stmt-lem-strip"></a>
<div id="lem:strip" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6** (Faithful strips). *Let $S,T$ be disjoint finite sets of coordinates. The subgroup generated by $x_{ij}(a)$ with $i\in S$, $j\in T$ maps isomorphically onto the additive group of matrices supported in $S\times T$ under $u\mapsto
\pi(u)-I$. A word supported on $S$ normalizes this subgroup and acts on its entries by left multiplication by its matrix on $S$. On the reverse strip $T\times S$, its action is right multiplication by the inverse matrix on $S$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-strip"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-strip</summary>

*Proof.* All roots in $S\times T$ commute by <a href="#eq:root-commuting" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:root-commuting">[eq:root-commuting]</a>. Root addition collects a word into one factor per coordinate pair. The corresponding entry matrices have pairwise zero products, so the matrix image reads each collected coefficient directly. This proves injectivity and identifies the group law with addition.

For one root supported on $S$, conjugation on an outgoing strip follows from <a href="#eq:root-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:root-product">[eq:root-product]</a>: it performs the corresponding elementary row addition. The same relations on the reverse strip perform the inverse elementary column operation. Iterating over the factors of a word gives the stated left and right actions, with their prescribed order over the possibly noncommutative algebra $B$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-stable-center"></a>
<div id="lem:stable-center" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (Stable centrality). *For every $B$, the group $J(B)$ is central in $\mathop{\mathrm{St}}(B)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-stable-center"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-stable-center</summary>

*Proof.* Let $z\in J(B)$ and choose a finite set $S$ containing the support of a representative of $z$. To show that $z$ commutes with a specified root, enlarge $S$ to contain both indices of that root. Choose $h\notin S$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:strip" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:strip">6</a>, $z$ acts trivially on the strips $S\times\{h\}$ and $\{h\}\times S$, since its matrix image is the identity. For $i,j\in S$ with $i\ne j$, the identity $$x_{ij}(a)=[x_{ih}(a),x_{hj}(1)]$$ therefore implies that $z$ commutes with $x_{ij}(a)$. These roots generate the group. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-permutations"></a>
<div id="lem:permutations" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8** (Coordinate permutations). *In characteristic two, every permutation of a finite set of coordinates is induced on roots by conjugation in the Steinberg group after adjoining an outside coordinate if necessary. In particular, relabeling the finitely many coordinates in a representative of $z\in J(B)$ leaves the element $z$ unchanged in $\mathop{\mathrm{St}}(B)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-permutations"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-permutations</summary>

*Proof.* For a transposition of coordinates $a,b$, use $$p=x_{ab}(1)x_{ba}(1)x_{ab}(1).$$ On a column strip from $\{a,b\}$ to an outside coordinate, its three elementary row additions exchange the two entries over $\mathbb F_2$. Lemma <a href="#lem:strip" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:strip">6</a> gives this as an exact group action. The reverse strip is exchanged in the same way. Roots with both indices outside $\{a,b\}$ commute with $p$. The remaining two root positions can be written, with $c\notin\{a,b\}$, as $$x_{ab}(u)=[x_{ac}(u),x_{cb}(1)],\qquad
 x_{ba}(u)=[x_{bc}(u),x_{ca}(1)].$$ The strip actions exchange these as well. Products of transpositions give arbitrary finite permutations. Any injective relabeling of a finite set extends to a permutation of a larger finite set, so the last assertion follows from Lemma <a href="#lem:stable-center" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:stable-center">7</a>. ◻

</details>

This proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:permutations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:permutations">8</a> is the argument of <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Companion](#ref-Companion), Lemma 6.3]; its exact action on roots, rather than only on their matrix images, will matter below.

<a id="stmt-lem-commutator"></a>
<div id="lem:commutator" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9** (A commutator identity). *In any group, let $A=[X,Y]$ and $B'=[Y,Z]$. If $$[X,Z]=[A,X]=[B',Z]=[A,B']=1,$$ then $[A,Z]=[X,B']$. This common commutator commutes with $A,Z,X,B'$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-commutator"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-commutator</summary>

*Proof.* The subgroups $H=\langle A,Z\rangle$ and $K=\langle X,B'\rangle$ commute elementwise by the four hypotheses. Conjugating $[X,Z]=1$ by $Y$ gives $$[A^{-1}X,B'Z]=1.$$ Separating the commuting $H$ and $K$ factors yields $[A^{-1},Z][X,B']=1$. Thus $[X,B']=[A^{-1},Z]^{-1}$ lies in $H\cap K$, which is central in both $H$ and $K$. Centrality in $H$ then gives $[A,Z]=[A^{-1},Z]^{-1}=[X,B']$. ◻

</details>

Finally, $\mathop{\mathrm{St}}_n(B)$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{E}}_n(B)$ are perfect for $n\ge3$: each generating root is the commutator in <a href="#eq:root-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:root-product">[eq:root-product]</a> with one coefficient equal to $1$. If $B$ is infinite, both groups are infinite, since distinct coefficients at a fixed root position give distinct elementary matrices.

<a id="sec:shifts"></a>
### Cyclic shifts and the relative central kernel

We now study elements of $J(R)$ whose reduction to $\mathop{\mathrm{St}}(\mathbb F_2)$ is trivial. The grading lets us evaluate such an element at a finite numerical matrix. We shall prove that evaluation at a sufficiently long cyclic shift is trivial, and that evaluation at a cyclic shift of $2$-power size is a power of the original element. These two identities imply $2$-primary torsion in the relative central kernel. Later we will compare the first identity with an odd power.

The matrix substitutions are elementary versions of the graded transfer operations of Weibel <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Weibel1987](#ref-Weibel1987), Section 1]. At the level of stable algebraic $K$-theory, the relative torsion conclusion also follows from his continuous Witt-vector action <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Weibel1987](#ref-Weibel1987), Theorem 0.1]. We reproduce the concrete Steinberg-group arguments of the companion <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Companion](#ref-Companion), Lemmas 6.4–6.7], since their exact identities, rather than identities of matrix images, are needed here.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Block roots and triangular evaluation

For the next three lemmas, let $B$ be any unital $\mathbb F_2$-algebra. Fix $n\geq3$ and $b\geq1$, and label $nb$ coordinates by $(i,s)$, where $1\leq i\leq n$ and $0\leq s<b$. We call $i$ the outer index and $s$ the inner index. For $A=(a_{st})\in\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}_b(B)$ and $i\ne j$, define the *block root* $$X_{ij}(A)=\prod_{s,t=0}^{b-1}
           x_{(i,s),(j,t)}(a_{st})\in\mathop{\mathrm{St}}_{nb}(B).$$ All factors commute, because their source outer index is $i$ and their target outer index is the different index $j$.

<a id="stmt-lem-block-laws"></a>
<div id="lem:block-laws" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10** (Block roots and coordinate changes). *The block roots satisfy the Steinberg relations over $\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}_b(B)$: $$\begin{aligned}
 X_{ij}(A+A')&=X_{ij}(A)X_{ij}(A'),\\
 [X_{ij}(A),X_{kl}(A')]&=1
       &&\text{if }j\ne k\text{ and }i\ne l,\\
 [X_{ij}(A),X_{jk}(A')]&=X_{ik}(AA')
       &&\text{if }i,j,k\text{ are distinct}.
\end{aligned}$$ Consequently they define a homomorphism $\mathop{\mathrm{St}}_n(\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}_b(B))\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{St}}_{nb}(B)$. Moreover, for every $P\in\mathop{\mathrm{GL}}_b(\mathbb F_2)$ there is an element $q\in\mathop{\mathrm{St}}_{nb}(B)$ such that $$q^{-1}X_{ij}(A)q=X_{ij}(P^{-1}AP)
 \qquad(i\ne j).$$ Its matrix image is the block diagonal matrix with a copy of $P$ in every outer block.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-block-laws"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-block-laws</summary>

*Proof.* Additivity and the commuting relation follow factor by factor. For the consecutive commutator, the only nontrivial elementary commutators are $$[x_{(i,s),(j,t)}(a_{st}),
   x_{(j,t),(k,v)}(a'_{tv})]
   =x_{(i,s),(k,v)}(a_{st}a'_{tv}).$$ Each resulting root commutes with both input blocks. Expanding the commutator and using additivity at each position therefore gives the entries $\sum_t a_{st}a'_{tv}$ of $AA'$.

An elementary operation supported inside outer block $i$ acts on the outgoing strip to block $j$ by the corresponding row operation; an operation inside block $j$ acts by the inverse column operation. These are exact strip actions by Lemma <a href="#lem:strip" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:strip">6</a>. An operation inside any other outer block centralizes the block root. Gaussian elimination over $\mathbb F_2$ writes $P$ as a product of elementary matrices: every nonzero pivot is $1$, and a row transposition is a product of three row additions. Lift those factors inside each outer block. Applying the strip actions successively gives the asserted conjugation. For $b=1$, $P=1$ and the assertion is immediate. ◻

</details>

If $v(t)$ is a root word over the polynomial algebra $B[t]$, with $t$ central, and $T\in\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}_b(\mathbb F_2)$, let $W_v(T)$ be its evaluation at $t=T$, followed by the block-root homomorphism. The substitution $B[t]\to\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}_b(B)$ is a unital algebra homomorphism: the numerical matrix $T$ commutes with every scalar matrix from $B$. In particular, if $v(t)$ has identity matrix image over $B[t]$, then $W_v(T)$ has identity matrix image over $B$. For each inner index $s$, also write $$\iota_s:\mathop{\mathrm{St}}_n(B)\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{St}}_{nb}(B),\qquad
 x_{ij}(a)\longmapsto x_{(i,s),(j,s)}(a).$$ These coordinate homomorphisms have commuting images for distinct $s$; we do not assume they are injective.

<a id="stmt-lem-triangular"></a>
<div id="lem:triangular" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:triangular-evaluation"></a>
**Lemma 11** (Triangular evaluation). *Let $U$ be generated by the roots $x_{(i,s),(j,t)}(a)$ with $s<t$, allowing $i=j$. Then $U$ injects into its matrix image and is normalized by every root whose inner indices agree. If a root word $v(t)$ over $B[t]$ has identity matrix image, and $T\in\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}_b(\mathbb F_2)$ is upper triangular with constant diagonal entry $\lambda$, then $$\label{eq:triangular-evaluation}
 W_v(T)=\prod_{s=0}^{b-1}\iota_s\bigl(v(\lambda)\bigr).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-triangular"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-triangular</summary>

*Proof.* The *gap* of a generator of $U$ is $t-s>0$. Two such roots either commute or have a commutator root whose gap is the sum of their gaps, by the consecutive-root relations in the two possible orders. They cannot be opposite roots. For $g\geq1$, let $U_g$ be generated by roots of gap at least $g$; thus $U_1=U$ and $U_b=1$. Conjugating a generator of $U_g$ by a generator of $U$ adds only roots of larger gap, so $U_g$ is normal in $U$. Modulo $U_{g+1}$, the group $U_g$ is generated by the commuting roots of gap $g$.

It follows, successively for $g=1,\ldots,b-1$, that every element of $U$ is an ordered product with one factor at each coordinate pair, in increasing gap; repeated coefficients at a given pair are added. This is a finite collection process, since there are finitely many coordinate pairs and $U_b=1$. If its matrix image is the identity, the entries of gap one are precisely the corresponding collected coefficients, so they vanish. After coefficients of smaller gap have vanished, the same assertion holds at gap $g$: any product of two remaining entry matrices has gap greater than $g$. Induction makes every coefficient zero and proves faithfulness.

A root with equal inner indices cannot be opposite to a positive-gap root. Its commutator with a positive-gap root is either trivial or has the same positive gap. This proves that it normalizes $U$.

For a polynomial entry $a(t)$, the matrix $a(T)$ is upper triangular with diagonal entries $a(\lambda)$. Split its block root into its commuting diagonal and strictly upper factors. The diagonal factors normalize $U$, so the strictly upper factors in the complete word can be collected on the right. Diagonal factors at different inner indices commute, giving $$W_v(T)=\left(\prod_s\iota_s(v(\lambda))\right)u,
 \qquad u\in U.$$ The left side and all the words $v(\lambda)$ have identity matrix image. Hence $u$ has identity matrix image. Faithfulness of $U$ gives $u=1$ and proves <a href="#eq:triangular-evaluation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:triangular-evaluation">[eq:triangular-evaluation]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Removing the wrap of a cyclic shift

Let $C_b$ be the numerical cyclic shift from row $s$ to column $s+1$ modulo $b$, and let $S_b$ be the shift with the same entries except for the entry from row $b-1$ to column $0$, which is removed. Thus $S_b$ is strictly upper triangular. The next lemma replaces $C_b$ by $S_b$ without changing the evaluated Steinberg element. Its proof confines the difference to a faithful strip between two disjoint bands of inner indices.

<a id="stmt-lem-wrap"></a>
<div id="lem:wrap" class="lemma">

**Lemma 12** (Removing the wrap). *Let $v(t)$ be a product of $m\geq1$ root factors over $B[t]$, whose polynomial entries have degrees at most $d\geq0$. Suppose its matrix image is the identity over $B[t]$. Put $\delta=\max\{1,d\}$. If $$b>2(m+1)\delta,$$ then $W_v(C_b)=W_v(S_b)$ in $\mathop{\mathrm{St}}_{nb}(B)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-wrap"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-wrap</summary>

*Proof.* For each factor, split its cyclic evaluation as $D_hF_h$, where $D_h$ is its evaluation at $S_b$ and $F_h$ consists of the entries that wrap past inner index $b-1$. The splitting is exact because all roots in one outer rectangle commute. Since $d<b$, a term of degree $a\leq d$ goes from $s$ to $s+a$ and can wrap only once. Every root in $F_h$ therefore has source in $\{b-\delta,\ldots,b-1\}$ and target in $\{0,\ldots,\delta-1\}$.

For $r\geq1$, set $$H_r=\{b-r\delta,\ldots,b-1\},\qquad
 L_r=\{0,\ldots,r\delta-1\}.$$ When $b>2r\delta$, the roots from inner indices in $H_r$ to inner indices in $L_r$, with arbitrary outer indices, form an abelian strip $V_r$ that injects into matrices by Lemma <a href="#lem:strip" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:strip">6</a>. Figure <a href="#fig:wrap-strip" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:wrap-strip">1</a> displays these two bands. We shall show that conjugation by one $D_h$ enlarges their widths by at most $\delta$.

<figure id="fig:wrap-strip">

<figcaption>The correction strip $V_{r}$ goes from the right band $H_{r}$ to the left band $L_{r}$; each band includes every outer index. The proof shows that one truncated block factor moves each endpoint by at most $δ$. The bound $b > 2(m+1)δ$ keeps the enlarged bands disjoint throughout the collection.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-wrap-strip"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:wrap-strip`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=0.95cm,y=0.8cm,>=Stealth]
 \fill[gray!15] (0,0) rectangle (2,0.65);
 \fill[gray!30] (8,0) rectangle (10,0.65);
 \draw (0,0) rectangle (10,0.65);
 \draw (2,0)--(2,0.65) (8,0)--(8,0.65);
 \node at (1,0.325) {$L_r$};
 \node at (9,0.325) {$H_r$};
 \draw[->] (9,0.85)--(9,1.4)--(1,1.4)--(1,0.85);
 \node at (5,1.77) {correction roots};
 \node[below] at (0,0) {$0$};
 \node[below] at (2,0) {$r\delta-1$};
 \node[below] at (8,0) {$b-r\delta$};
 \node[below] at (10,0) {$b-1$};
\end{tikzpicture}
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 11 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A horizontal schematic with two shaded rectangular intervals along a baseline. The left rectangle is labeled $L_r$, with tick marks labeled $0$ at its left endpoint and $r\delta - 1$ at its right endpoint. The right rectangle is labeled $H_r$, with tick marks labeled $b - r\delta$ at its left endpoint and $b - 1$ at its right endpoint. A leftward-pointing arrow extends from the left side of $H_r$ to the right side of $L_r$, labeled "correction roots" directly above it.
```


<a id="eq:strip-enlargement"></a>
More precisely, for $r\leq m$ we claim that $$\label{eq:strip-enlargement}
 D_h^{-1}V_rD_h\subseteq V_{r+1}.$$ Fix a root of $V_r$. The constituents of $D_h$ all have the same outer source $i$ and outer target $j\ne i$, and inner displacements between $0$ and $d$. They commute with one another. A constituent that commutes with the initial root also commutes with every conjugate of it by the other constituents, and so can be omitted from this conjugation.

An interacting constituent changes the source endpoint only when its target is the old source; the new source is at most $d$ positions to the left. It changes the target endpoint only when its source is the old target; the new target is at most $d$ positions to the right.
